The above regulation can be seen, ladies and gentlemen, as having, in particular, a festive content - some will say so - but it will be attempted, in other words, by raising this issue in a constitutional context to the institutions of the State, the importance attached by the constitutional legislature to this particular state obligation. I feel just here that I need to formulate a position of principle on social rights when these are provided for in the Constitution. Provisions specific to social rights already in existence in the Constitution and the content of which case law has been formulated for decades should not be reviewed. The nature and the nature of social rights, especially with regard to the possibility of relying on them by the governors, obliges us as a review legislator to express ourselves with enthusiasm. That is to say, the demagogic majorities of social rights must not be placed in the constitutional text. This is because the dependence of the satisfaction of social rights on the economic situation in which a country is situated obliges the editorial or revised legislators to draw up the constitutional provisions on social rights with pitiful and not demagogic. We need to understand it well that the more generous the constitutional provisions on social rights are, the more likely life can turn them, as I have said, into political declarations or wishes. When relevant cases, based on well-established constitutional provisions, are brought to the courts, then it is likely that legal arrangements at the top of the sources of law do not constitute the legal basis for accepting the demands of which the governors claim satisfaction and then prove that a provision of constitutional law does not produce legal effects. To reach agreement, I would like to remind you of a phrase by King Ferrari: 'Public subscriptions and rewards are a sacred debt of the country', and obviously, ladies and gentlemen, he did not mean wishes. A few more words about the theory of social rights. And I will refer to my infamous professor Dimitris Chachos who, with his then collaborative and now professor of Constitutional Law Neda Kanellopoulou, wrote about social rights, that freedom through them has entered into a new phase. It is not only through individual rights, where the citizen requires the State to refrain from a sphere that is active or simply through civil rights, where the citizen demands to take part in the decision-making, but also with the demands of social security. It is therefore about the social dimension of freedom, namely the passage from the liberal state to the social state and, ultimately, the social rule of law, the social orientation of power. But the economic crisis has taught us a great deal. In addition, the theories of the social acquis were abolished. This does not mean, however, that the hard core of the social state has been eliminated, that social rights have in practice been abolished. However, those who defended the theories of the social acquis between 2009 and 2014 proved to be inappropriate, in the end, or in the worst case conscious demagogues in the field of law. We must therefore not accept unrecognised experience of both the theory and practice of Constitutional Law, especially when we are in the Chamber of a Review House and draft the texts that the official interpreter of the Constitution will be asked to interpret. I repeat that every change in our country's top law must be done with enthusiasm, rather than reviewing it, just to show work. Also, it is not just possible to level everything on the argument of neutrality. And I'm explaining. SYRIZA supports the constitutional ratification of the political oath as an expression of the religious neutrality of the state. He considers that, if the religious oath is mandatory, it is contrary to religious freedom. However, ladies and gentlemen, under the pretext of religious neutrality, we cannot attempt to revise other parts of the Constitution, which have a different purpose and regulate - if you like - different objects. For example, the qualities of the President of the Republic, who is the supreme ruler of the Greek state, have nothing to do with the exercise of individual and social rights, but with the exercise of acts of state sovereignty and representation of our country. In this context, therefore, the Greek Solution firmly believes that the President of the Republic should give only religious vow before taking up his duties, because his duties have not only a prestige, but also a supreme responsibility towards the country and the Greeks, which, by definition, only entail religious vows. I hope we all understand in this House that the religious oath of the President of the Republic is not an expression of negative religious freedom. It is an expression of the high sense of responsibility and faith towards the country that the President of the Republic must have. I would like to focus on individual and social rights, as they are shaped by the crown of the rule of law in our country. In this way, it universally protects the life, honour and freedom of all those on Greek territory, without any discrimination. All the politicians on the planet must be more brave when it comes to the organisation and functioning of their states, with the sole aim of strengthening national societies against aggressive globalisation. The conflict is, therefore, between the global uncontrolled economic system, which seeks to eliminate national borders, to free the capital, to move freely on goods and work, and the people who defend the history and tradition of their cultures. So the right is the one fighting for the free movement of capital and goods and the new left, which has become politically unemployed since the collapse of the Eastern bloc, is fighting for the free movement of slave labour and freedom of immigration. So, in the dominant ideology of globalisation, of this 'open society', which we so often hear, namely relaxing, passive society and open and uncontrolled borders, we give up the ideology of active, dynamic, strong society, which will ensure the integration of human beings, prosperity and security of its members. For example, the right to strike is respected, as is all rights. But it cannot outweigh it at the expense of the good of health. Citizens' abuse of rights should not be allowed. In a similar context, Ireland has already made progress in Article 45(2) of its constitution and Romania in Article 47(1). I'm going to the section of Article 22. Our rapporteur, in the House proposed, the current President of Parliament, Mr Tasoulas, called this complex of provisions of Article 22 "A Constitution of Happiness". How is that supposed to be? The proposal for counter-population contains self-evident rights, which in any case are enshrined in both our European and our constitutional framework, such as the age-based prohibition, Article 14 of the ECHR, the European Convention on Human Rights, Article 19 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the Union, Article 21 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights, Directive 78/2000, as ratified in 2016 by the Greek Parliament. The SYRIZA proposal also contains ideologically charged wishes, which have been judged nationally and institutionally at European and international level. In particular, the proposal for unilateral recourse by the social partners to arbitration and for limiting its object solely to the definition of basic salary is contrary to the Constitution, Article 22(2) as the Greek Council of State told us by Decision 2307/2014. Internationally speaking, for voluntary negotiations, the International Convention 98 of 1949 of the International Labour Organisation, as ratified by Greece in 1961, and also the Convention 151 and 154 of the International Labour Organisation. Finally, the European Social Charter, as ratified by Article 6, in other words the right to collective bargaining, stresses: 'In order to ensure the effective exercise of the right to collective bargaining, the Contracting Parties undertake, firstly, to promote equal consultation between employers and workers, secondly, to promote, where necessary and appropriate, voluntary negotiation between employers and workers and, thirdly, to promote the establishment and use of appropriate trading and arbitration procedures to regulate labour disputes'. The SYRIZA proposal is therefore completely outside the international and European framework, which also applies and binds our country anyway. The proposal, which relates to Article 22(4), to ban the taking up of services as a measure to deal with the consequences of the strike, is also utopian. In this respect, the Plenary Assembly of the Council of State has expressed a document in its decision of 1623/2012 on the provision of vital services to society as a whole and on the protection of public health. A similar balance has also been retained by the joint legislator in Law No.4325/2015 and Law No. 536/2007. The constitutionalisation of the requirement ban, in the event of prolonged strikes which disrupt social life, may create serious risks in society. This is also acknowledged by the International Labour Organisation at the 81st International Labour Conference, with the Declaration made in Geneva, Switzerland, in 1994. Finally, the realisation of a social security system proposed by SYRIZA in Article 22(5) will cause insurmountable problems if it is faced with the lack of resources, due to unemployment or demographic. The existing provision is sufficiently general and therefore allows the joint legislator to adapt the insurance system to changing economic conditions, equilibriuming, as in most states, between distribution and capitalisation. This is also enshrined in Decision 2180/2004 of the Council of State, which explicitly recognises the broad power of the joint legislature, in the context of the Constitution at all times. And do not forget the recent decisions, which considered the main anti-constitutional provisions of Law4387/2016 to be unconstitutional. Finally, with regard to SYRIZA's proposal to abolish the provision of Article 25(3) on the abuse of a right, it is in direct conflict with the protection of the core of rights, because by definition abusive is the exercise of a right, when it is made for a manifestly different purpose than the one for which it was established. It is also clear that the proposal to repeal is directly conflicting with European and international law, which guarantees it, which, of course, Greece has ratified, such as Article 17 on the prohibition of abuse of the right of the European Convention on Human Rights, Article 54 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union, and the judgment of the European Court of Human Rights 2009 ‘Miroliubov and other versus Latvia’. Several countries provide for the abuse of a right, even in their constitution, such as Germany in Article 18 of the Grundgesetz. The principle of leniency, which is invoked by the Opposition, is an vague theoretical and legal concept, which will clearly create legal uncertainty. On the other hand, a legally confiscated concept is the principle of proportionality, as set out in Article 25(1) of the Constitution, and has also been defined by the Council of State in Decision 2195/2005 and 3665/2005, as well as by the Court of Justice of the European Union in the judgment ‘Handyside versus United Kingdom’ in 1976. Because we refuse to overload the Constitution by means of provisions which make it stiff and can even disorientate it against our common European code by adding, and not by resolving issues, we refuse to vote on these changes, proposed by SYRIZA, and we insist on a Constitution that is worthwhile but open to European developments. Thank you.

The Americans, for example, in their constitution have an article that states that the government cannot deprive any Americans of the right to seek happiness. The particular features of a nation are, therefore, that distinguish a constitution from an international treaty on its content. Otherwise, there would probably be no need for constitutions by state, and in a society of internationalist, the rules of the United Nations or the Rome Convention or the universal human rights rules would be enough. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u98	Mr President, I shall refer to Articles 5, 21, 22 and 25 on social and individual rights. I'll be specific. I will mention the proposal from the New Democracy and a comment - criticism of the proposals from the other parties on these articles. The biggest acquis of the 2001 Revision is, without doubt, Article 25(1), the social state, the epicity of the rules of interpretation of the Constitution in the field of social rights. SYRIZA uses the Revision of the Constitution at the level of social rights for constitutional rhetoric alone. Because everything that is proposed in relation to social rights is already guaranteed by the existing constitution. They are interpreted by the existing constitutional wordings and supplemented or specified in international texts such as the European Social Charter, or by a number of European Union regulations and directives. Constitutional rhetoric, demagogy and constitutional populism risk turning the Constitution into a declaratory text, without a practical reflection, undermining its own legal supremacy. The application of social rights, which constitute benefits at budgetary cost, is subject to the actual condition of budgetary capacity and, in particular, to the country's budgetary strength and substance. When we talk about social rights, we cannot take into account the financial conditions governing the exercise of social rights - see Heliopoulou - and the European Convention on Human Rights, the European Court of Human Rights and the European legal order. I'm coming to the articles. In Article 5(2), the existing constitutional provisions fully guarantee the protection of life, honor and freedom, without prejudice to any discrimination either in terms of sex or in terms of identity, ethnicity, religion, age, or even sexual orientation. The revision of Article 5(2) in the proposed form is therefore considered to be surplus and irreversible. In support of this, I refer to Resolution 67/2018 of the Amarusian Court on the right of self-determination. In Article 21(1) - the article on minimum guaranteed income, which is the proposal of the New Democracy - the minimum guaranteed income comes from the principle of a social state and is the ratio of all social rights. SYRIZA's proposal to include the minimum guaranteed income in Article 21, without naming it, unfairly and discrediting the value of social rights, thus confirming today's negative vote by SYRIZA - I would remind you - in voting on the minimum guaranteed income in the Greek Parliament in 2012, as a trap of poverty. The right to the minimum guaranteed income works defenceally, i.e. prevents the reduction of benefits below a limit which would jeopardise the decent living of the individual and his family. It is a targeted social provision, which is granted under income criteria, so that no citizen is found below the poverty line. In other countries, it is paid as a difference between the beneficiary's individual family income and is incorporated in other social protection institutions and in other countries as a certain amount, autonomous benefit, since the beneficiary's income is below the legally determined limits. In France, for example, the income of active solidarity, as it is said, was first established in 2009 and is identified as a supplement to labour income for people with inadequate professional income. But what needs to be stressed is that the minimum guaranteed income must also be accompanied by the process of social reintegration and combating exclusion so that it does not become a lifestyle. It should be transitional in nature and be accompanied by social care and employment promotion measures. Article 21(3): The existing Article 21(3) of the Constitution establishes the obligation of the State for the health of citizens, i.e. the obligation to take positive measures, without implying that the State has a monopoly on the organisation and operation of the provision of health services. See the decision of the Council of State 1374/1997 not to introduce the State Health Service monopoly. With Article 21, in the current official view, the editorial legislator gives the power to the joint legislator to regulate the social right to health protection. The legislator has the obligation of the Constitution to regulate and, in particular, to ensure the protection of the health of citizens and to take specific measures for certain categories of the population, which require increased protection, such as people with disabilities, over-aged people, multi-texts. Here, on behalf of SYRIZA, there is an attempt to ensure that an exclusive state, universal standard of health provision, a work of the Constitution for ideological reasons, is carried out. The Constitution cannot reflect the ideological desire of each majority, but it should be simple, without anachronistic thoughts and ideologies and, of course, it cannot ignore the reality of the functioning of the mixed system of health services, which prevails in Greece. It is clear that for SYRIZA stateers, the role and reason of the public is confused with the state-of-the-art health system, since they cannot understand that the nature of the provider - state or private - cannot alter the public nature of the right to health. Article 21(7): On the basis of the concepts of the State and of the productive forces developed in it, we have on the one hand the theory that the State sets the rules and ensures the control mechanisms, regardless of whether the provider is the State itself or private, while, on the other hand, we have the view of a hydro-headed state, which is trying to successfully control the production and exploitation of goods, without guaranteeing the provision of quality services to citizens. We clearly choose the first version. The hypocritical reason for those who grant parts of the utilities is unbearable demagogic, as they move to constitutional rhetoric, simply mocking some left reflexes, at the same time as, as a government, they were leading to a peculiar left privatization. Reminds L.4336/2015 with FEK A94, Article 7, subparagraph b2 entitled ‘Electricity Regulations’, page 953. It is also timely, because we are also discussing PPC in the Committee on Production and Trade. Article 22(1): The principle of equal treatment at work is a major issue. For this reason, the existing constitutional provision already prohibits any form of unjustified discrimination in respect of remuneration. But the constitutional safeguarding of equal rights and obligations - Article 4(2) of the Constitution - excludes all forms of discrimination. Similar discrimination in the workplace is those associated with origin, political and religious beliefs, disability, etc. The specificity of the prohibition on discrimination, because of age, hides a dangerous surplus, since it essentially targetes the young people who are under-aged up to twenty-five years and over fifty years, with the sole aim of working for the Constitution, for the benefit of party ideas. It limits drastic working relations, such as those of the trainee and the tester, which are now fully recognised at European level. Article 22(2): The possibility of self-regulation of trade union organisations is an important expression of modern democracy, of the right of assembly - Article 12(1) and (3) - of freedom of expression - Article 5(3) - of the free development of personality referred to in Article 5(1). The trade union representatives can unnecessarily regulate labour relations without state interference through free collective bargaining. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] In the same spirit, moreover, the ECHR moves to Article 11, the European Social Charter in Article 6 and international labour conventions which guarantee the principles of free collective bargaining. The arbitration procedure, as a mechanism for regulating labour relations, cannot in any case replace free collective bargaining through the unilateral recourse of the social partners to it. The current scheme, which must be maintained, is, in our view, the free collective bargaining and the subsidiary arbitral resolution of labour disputes. Article 22(4): The remit of the personal services or, as has been said, the political backing and supply of work within local government, that is, social work, are exceptions to the ban on forced labour. The complete abolition of the requirement in so-called'social and national solidarity' cases means that there are no risks of social cohesion and the state's proper functioning, so that there is a last point of defence in possible emergencies, such as war, confiscation, defence needs of the country, natural disasters and reasons for public health. Article 22(5): Care for the social security of workers in Greece is expressed in this article. It follows from the wording of Article 22(5) that social security is the purpose of the State in which the joint legislature is required to specify the content of the social security right, without any constitutional distinction, between voluntary insurance, based on the agreement of the persons concerned and compulsory insurance, which is based on the law. It is the joint legislator who is obliged to set up a framework of mechanisms to promote the functioning of the social security institution. Furthermore, the existing constitutional legislation does not, in the first place, impose specific restrictions on the way in which legal persons become social security institutions, since such a commitment by the joint legislature does not result either from the spirit of the Constitution or from the general principles deriving from the constitutional provisions, which establish the 'Social State principle', i.e. Article 25. The aim of the constitutional enforcement of the way in which socio-security policy is formulated is a classic ideological and anachronistic concept of controlling social, economic and development processes. Invoking social solidarity and the constitutional system of redistribution when SYRIZA, as a government, wiped out seventy thousand widow's pensions, captures the hypocritical and demagogue speech, which tries to enclose a constitutional formula. Ensuring the sustainability and effectiveness of social security in the light of the international practice of mixed social security systems - redistributive and capitalising - demonstrate the great potential and scope of social security legislation. It is clear that the joint legislator should not be confined by the Constitution to finding a sustainable and long-lasting population insurance coverage. An ideological and timely constitutional forecast like the one proposed by SYRIZA can lead to the so-called ideological cleanliness of the constitutional legislature, but at the same time can lead to an empty insurance fund and a social deadlock of millions of pensioners. Article 25(3): The Constitution, as a supreme defender of individual and social rights, is the exclusive rule, which may prohibit their abuse. The limits between fair use and unfair abuse are very short and arise from the purpose of the constitutional right, which is to be overhauled every time. Furthermore, Article 25(2) positively defines the declining concept of abuse in the sense that the development of a fundamental right, in a way that is incompatible with social progress within the framework of freedom and justice, is a abuse and is prohibited.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u114	Thank you, Mr President. I will focus on my position on social and labour rights and I would like to start with two main assumptions: The first admission is about social rights themselves. Social rights - and we want this for our part to stress this - are not rights of passive people to benefits from the state. That is not, above all, social rights. They are not rights of passive subjects for transferring payments from the State to them. Social rights are rights of active subjects, they are rights to participate in social, economic and political life, they are rights to collective claims and to participate in the generated wealth. That is why the uterus of social rights is the right to work. This is because the right to work can guarantee this biopolitic level so that people, citizens, the rights providers can collectively participate and claim, but also because the basic expression of the right to work, collective autonomy, collective bargaining, that is to say, are a manifest indication of this participation, of this collective claim and of the involvement of the wealth produced. The Italian Constitution says that very clearly. In Article 1 it says that Italy is a democratic state based on work. And I think that that is clearly what I just said. The second admission, which I believe is important and crucial to enable us to continue the debate, is this: in general terms, in the field of rights - not only social rights - the Constitution is a multi-theistic universe, that is, it has many principles and values of equal formality, which, in practice, are very often conflicting with each other. The judge then intervenes so that he can resolve this real conflict and make this weighting and practical harmonisation of conflicting sizes. A very characteristic example is that the right to work often conflicts - and there have been cases like this often in the courts - with the right to protect the environment, that is, an economic activity which can be regarded as having harmful effects on the environment and at the same time workers want to continue this activity because they are guaranteed the right to work. This is a classic case of practical conflict. It is a conflict, in fact, of values and rights which exist in the Constitution and which have, first and foremost, a level playing field. And there comes the judge to make this weighting and practical harmonisation, in this case, that is to say, demarcate or constrain the extent to which a right is satisfied and allow the other person to be more satisfied. This fact, that may, on a case-by-case basis, be less or more satisfying than that of one or other right, does not mean that we may have the suspension of a social or labour right as a whole. That would be unacceptable constitutional. It is unacceptable constitutionally that the validity of a right should be fully suspended. I want to say this because there is a very long debate - I have heard it from the previous speakers - in particular with regard to social rights, that it is the budgetary conditions that actually define the functioning of a social right. It's not like that. The budgetary conditions may change the degree of satisfaction of a right, but in no case is it permitted to cancel entirely the validity of a right recognised by the Constitution. And this, unfortunately, has happened too many times in the near future, especially in terms of labour rights. In other words, in the second Memorandum in particular, we had a clear violation of rights, which either directly stemming from the Constitution or from the case-law and theory have crystallised them as the content of the Constitution. I say this because I have also heard Mr Loverdo - you said not to mention names, but not sometimes - to say that the provisions which have long been formulated in terms of their content either of case law or of the theory, or of both, should not, therefore, change what SYRIZA proposes in many articles, especially in social rights, are demagogic mafia. In such a climate was the position of the Special Rapporteur of the New Democracy, who said they were constitutional rhetoric, demagogy, populism, etc. The problem is that in the field of social and labour rights, despite the fact that the theory and case law have long been crystallised by their content - so part of Mr Loverdou's position is entirely valid - in practice they have been breached by the ordinary legislator. It therefore appears that we absolutely need a better shield and an update of the content of these provisions. The present version of these rights was, however, not sufficient to shield them from insults by the joint legislator and by the second memorandum, but only shortly after that. So the problem, in my opinion, is not whether this is a matter of malignancies or of a physiological nature or whether it is populism or if it is more words to make a impression, etc. The issue, after all, is what Mr Katrugalos said. The issue here is political choice. And in the field of social and labour rights, that is the issue, that is where, in the clearest terms, the intersectional political approaches we have for the social, economic and labour model we want in this place are manifested. It is therefore a matter of difference in substance and not of wording. If it were a matter of wording, I do not think you would have a problem accepting the proposals we are making. In particular - and the happy thing is that social and employment rights are so clear that a non-legislative can understand exactly what this is about and where the disagreement lies - we propose, for example, Article 22.1 of the Constitution to guarantee an explicit equal pay for equal work regardless of age. This right and theory and case law have been brought together for decades from the present wording of the article. However, this was not enough to prevent the 2012 legislator from coming and establishing the underpaid wage, that is, the exact distinction between wages and age. So it seemed that the Constitution was not sufficient to inhibit such an insult. We say that this ban on discrimination must be clearly guaranteed and the New Democracy seems to reject this proposal. So every citizen thinks he can draw his conclusions. The second question concerns the fixing of minimum wages. Here too, the theory and case law for a long time have said that this is the result of the existing form of article of the Constitution, because it actually says that the state can only intervene in subsidiary terms, when, that is, there is no collective agreement between employers and employees and, in any case, the legislator cannot intervene and can in no way remove material from collective autonomy. And yet, although this had been crystallised for decades both from case law and from theory, in 2012 the Memorandum of Understanding again came to the second memorandum to remove material from collective bargaining, the determinant, the minimum wage, that is to say, to say that this will be determined by law and, indeed, to reduce it very much. We say that it should be explicitly guaranteed that the minimum wage is laid down by national general collective agreements. I can prevent a counter-project, which may be heard - the JRC often says - that 'yes, but SYRIZA made use of this possibility of increasing wages by law'. He didn't bring him to the social partners himself. He raised him by law. Look, I think our position is very reasonable. The state cannot intervene, protect the object from collective bargaining, reduce the wage dramatically and then come, return it and say, 'now negotiate'. If he did that, he would have put a bomb at the bottom of the negotiation to the detriment of the workers. It is the state's duty to restore the level of wages where it was found, i.e. 751, and then, in our view, to return it to free collective bargaining. And that is exactly what we started to implement. This proposal seems to be rejected by the New Democracy and I think that no one can draw its conclusions. The other issue - also very serious - is the principle of more favourable regulation. What does this principle say, which we say should be explicitly enshrined in the Constitution? It says, basically, that sectoral collective agreements will prevail mainly and only when an enterprise provides for better wages and working conditions, then it will prevail. This proposal is rejected by the New Democracy. It is not to be surprised that this is because in 2012 this same principle was affected by the second memorandum and led to an explosion of operational collective agreements and a reduction in wages. The same is happening now, with the development law that the government adopted some time ago. Once again, a direct attack on the principle of more favourable regulation. But there's something more serious. And it's interesting, because we're talking right now and the PPC bill to the committees. It seems, contrary to what Mr Javaras said, that, in other words, New Democracy gives priority to the operational level that is closer to regulating working conditions, that ultimately does not give priority to the operational level, because now PPC is acting by law to abolish the existing business collective agreement, because this operational collective agreement provides for good and increased wages. So, finally, we only like business collective agreements when they reduce wages. We don't like it differently. There is, therefore, a general veil of hypocritical importance here in relation to the issue of labour rights. The last issue in employment concerns the right to unilateral referral to arbitration. There, for a long time, and jurisprudence and theory accepted that this right stems directly from the current constitutional provision. That did not prevent the legislator again from abolishing unilateral referrals to arbitration. The Council of State came and said that the abolition is unconstitutional. He said that the purpose of the Constitution is distorted if the right to unilateral recourse to arbitration is not shielded. It annulled legislative intervention and forced the administration to rehabilitate a little later on with the Government's development law. The right to unilateral referral to arbitration has been effectively reintroduced. What does that look like? This shows that the Constitution on this issue must be clear and that this must be clearly captured, if we want another step, so that we can achieve collective labour agreements. However, I think that, as I said at the outset, we are not in agreement with the wording. The question is that we do not all want this direction in Parliament in regulating labour relations. To say two words about social security. I have heard the new Republic's rapporteur say that SYRIZA's proposal is ideological, short-term, time-consuming, etc. What do we say? As is the case with the social security provision, it has a very weak wording. It only says that the State ensures the social security of workers. And the Council of State has come in and come out of it, it has made an incredible moratorium and has made a series of beginnings, etc. We say first of all that we have a social right. There is no right to social security at the moment. It's got to be settled. Then, the basic principles of social security, which is public security, must be guaranteed - so you can only have social security - and the redistributive character - that is the reason why there must be a social security system, that is to say, to be able to redistribute income, in order to reduce social inequalities - both of which are at a reasonable balance, which is solidarity between generations in social security and rewardability, i.e. there is a reasonable relationship of contributions - benefits. These four things say they should be enshrined in the Constitution. Why does the New Democracy reject these fours? I think it is obvious that here, it is not the case that we want to constitutionalise Law No.4387, the Katorugalou law. We want to enter the Constitution four - five basic principles, which the cancellation court has imperiled as a result of which the result. Why shouldn't they be guaranteed there? What exactly is she worried about? Obviously, this would not allow the privatisation of social security. Definitely! So if our disagreement is there, it must be clearly called rather than hide behind verbal shapes. To close with the decent standard of living. We propose to Article 21, if I am not mistaken, that the State guarantee a universal decent standard of living and that guarantee that it will be carried out either through universal services, whether through income support or in combination with both. What we also say is that I think this anachronistic reference to Article 21.1 should be removed, that the state is caring for the family because it is the basis for the maintenance and promotion of the nation. The state must take care of the family and child because they are special groups of the population that have special needs and need special protection, not because it is the basis of nation reproduction. So this is an important part of our proposal. Global access to basic social goods, at the same time or even a minimum guaranteed income. These proposals - I am saying them briefly because, due to time, I have to close them - we have tabled them. The New Democracy's position, in my opinion, is revealing and I think that two things need to be maintained here: firstly, that the proposals in the field of social rights and labour rights have direct practical consequences for tens of thousands of our fellow citizens. So it is not a theoretical or philosophical debate. And the second is that the New Democracy's refusal to accept these basics I mentioned is also revealing the very specific working, economic and social model that would like to prevail in the country. Thank you very much.

The constitutionalisation of sexual self-determination and gender identity is a more special manifestation of the free development of personality enshrined in the first paragraph of Article 5. However, this provision of Article 5(1) contains a restriction that we must avoid. I mean good morals, which are a general clause which can, depending on the context, lead to a complete deterioration of specific personalities, especially those that stimulate the conservative reflexes of society. What is the real meaning of paragraph 2 in Article 5? It is that after you're self-determined under paragraph 1, it is forbidden to discriminate against what you have chosen to be. And the safeguarding of the absolute protection of life, honor and freedom without discrimination of sex, sexual orientation and gender identity is something that is now fully in line with social development, not only because women and men are approaching the status of full equality, but also because the members of the LGBTQ community can now develop their personality more freely than in the past. Ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to refer to the constitutional guarantee of a decent standard of living by guaranteeing the minimum guaranteed income. The minimum guaranteed income can be seen from both a political and a constitutional perspective. In political terms, the minimum guaranteed income can have beneficial effects both on the reduction of the consequences of extreme poverty and on the reintegration of those experiencing social exclusion. In order to have a minimum guaranteed income, however, there is a need for a dynamic economy and a strong social state. Today, however, the Greek economy is just now trying to recover from the effects of the ten-year crisis, while the social state, apart from bringing all the wounds of the Greek public administration, was also used by our political system as a pillar of customerism, mainly through the indiscriminate grant of all kinds of benefits to beneficiaries and non-beneficiaries indiscriminately. The guaranteeing of a decent standard of living is, of course, supported by the minimum guaranteed income. But it's not allowed to get stuck in it. The welfare state does not match income provision, it is much wider. It is primarily linked to the provision of social care services and, indeed, the modern trend is through the local government agencies. As a former mayor I feel the obligation to recall that during the economic crisis the municipalities were in the frontline of the welfare state and the welfare state, as a rule trying to help Greek and foreigners indiscriminately, all those who were facing the range of poverty and social exclusion. And that is just one of the many reasons why I insisted yesterday on one of the three major absentees of this revision, which is local government. The other two are education and public administration. It is worth reminding this House that the constitutional revision of Article 21(1) had been included as a proposal since December 2014, when the Ministry of Labour - Vassilis Kegeroglu will remember that - the national strategic framework for social inclusion, which was an integrated plan aimed at alleviating inequalities, modernising public integration policies and upgrading the cooperation framework of a state, a civil society and a market. The provision of minimum guaranteed income was within the pillars of the national strategy to combat poverty, access to services and the labour market inclusive. We must therefore guarantee the minimum guaranteed income, not under the terms of a poverty trap, but under the conditions of social, economic and political integration of the beneficiaries. Thank you, Mr President.

The remaining hundred and fifty three continued to operate, but in 1834 their assets entered a special administration and management regime in the ecclesiastical fund set up for this purpose. As regards the transitional status of land, from that of the Ottoman state to that of the modern Greek state, this was regulated for all cases by two royal decrees, 1833 and 1836. According to this, if claims were made by the monks of free Greece, they had to produce official Turkish documents, molds, proving the assignment to them by the official Turkish state of the sole quasi-consciousness of such land, which they did not. What did they do next period? The church was divesting, without any agreement with the state, which, for its part, also went on expropriation, but for the adequate needs of restoring the landowners and refugees. This function created a climate of constant disputes between both sides until 1952, which the Constitution authorised the government to expropriate land for the benefit of the deceased and livestock farmers by means of a transitional provision whereby the Greek Orthodox Church and the State concluded an agreement that was ratified by the State by decree, in which the government stated in essence that it was "the other of the constitutional rights referred to in Article 104 of the Constitution, with regard to the expropriation or forced leasing of the Church's property", but on the basis of this agreement the monies would sell up four-fifths of their farms and two-thirds of their pastures and receive 1/3 of their value. Nor has this constitutional arrangement been implemented as a whole. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u152	We will now give the floor to the Minister for Labour and Social Affairs, Mr Brutsi. Then, as we said, Mrs Xinoyannakopoulou, Mr Bogdanos, Mr Simopoulos, from the list of speakers, and then Mr Voutsis, will follow. Here, Minister, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u153	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I have the particular honour and pleasure of presenting myself to the Government before the National Representation the constitutional assurance in our Revision House of the minimum guaranteed income. This is a great social intervention. This is something that will change social policy in our country in the future as we knew it. But let me say that there is something I personally relate to. It was the period of 2012-2014, when at the time in the heart of the crisis, by monitoring and analysing the social policies that we were conducting as a Greek state, it was, unfortunately, found that the exercise of social policy in all previous years, as well as the good intention of having, as much resources as it could have - which we really had as a percentage of GDP as compared to the other European countries - ultimately the result was far below any expectation, because there were interactions and fragmentation of the system. We are talking about a system that did not see each other, so that resources would go lost in the sense that they would eventually lead to people who did not need the money and those who did not really have it, that they would not benefit. So already in 2012, with our insistence and monitoring on the problem of social policy, it has been established that the minimum guaranteed income can be part of social integration and one of the main pillars of social solidarity in Greece tomorrow. The minimum guaranteed income was proposed for the first time in Greece under Law 4093 of 2012. The first historic step of this social innovation was taken by the publication of the historical - I stress, a historical - joint ministerial decision under number 39892 of 2014, signed by Gikas Hardouvelis, Christos Stakos, George Dolios, Vassilis Kegeroglou and I, Yiannis Vroutzis, as Minister, and with which the legislative framework for beneficiaries was clarified, the level, the procedure and the bodies responsible for paying the minimum guaranteed income, as well as any other relevant topics for the implementation of this income support programme in some, initially, areas of Greek territory. The first step had already been taken. The crisis has emphatically demonstrated the distortions of the social protection system and the urgent need to introduce a minimum guaranteed income limit, which will guarantee human status first and foremost. The minimum guaranteed income is not just a mechanism for the recovery of extreme poverty, but is part of a wider supporting grid of social protection combined with social welfare services and reintegration measures in the labour market, because this is the most important objective. Inaugurating now in 2014 the institution of minimum guaranteed income as the most innovative social policy tool that the country has ever had, we have responded to the distortions, gaps and contradictions of the social protection system in Greece, which was socially unfair and mainly economic and socially ineffective. Today we are completing this intervention by carrying out the second and most important historic step, proposing its constitutional safeguard. The proposal for constitutional safeguards is a flagrant interference with social protection and the introduction of social rights, aimed at strengthening and supporting our economically weak fellow citizens who have been affected by the economic crisis. In this way, the weakest, hardest hit by the economic crisis, acquire an additional right and become visible for the first time in the constitutional order, while at the same time strengthening equality, proportionality, social rights and legal certainty. The minimum guaranteed income is already based on Article 21(3) of the Constitution "the principle of the social state", and Article 2 of the Constitution "respect for human value", while it appears more and more frequently in international texts: Article 13 of the European Social Charter, Article 30 of the Revised Charter, Article 34(3) of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union. Today the New Democracy, in its proposal to the Revision meeting of the Parliament for the constitutionalisation of the minimum guaranteed income, seeks to shield the protection of decent living while ensuring human dignity itself. In particular, in Article 21(1) of the Constitution, which refers to social rights, the New Democracy proposes to add: ‘The State shall ensure that all citizens enjoy decent living conditions by means of a minimum guaranteed income system’. In this way, we aim to strengthen social rights by enriching the Constitution with an additional right which is one of the most modern measures of social protection, along with strengthening other pillars of social protection, such as the institution of the family and access to the labour market for all. The minimum guaranteed income has gained a dominant position - because of the economic crisis - by establishing for all family members a minimum economic security, which is the basis for each society's cohesion. At the same time, however, it recommends an integrated intervention in the social fabric as it is accompanied by a wide range of support services aimed at actively reintegrating citizens into economic and social life. Today the minimum guaranteed income is for two hundred thousand households and covers a total of four hundred fifty thousand people. Of these fifty - six thousand are unemployed. In particular, twenty - one - parent families and over fifty - six thousand households with children are covered. Of all the families, two hundred and thirty - six thousand have incomes under 5,000 euros, while thirty - thousand have zero income. For this year, it is estimated that 670m euros will be paid. The average provision per household is EUR 213 monthly. Ladies and gentlemen, now is the golden opportunity for all the political forces to agree with this great constitutional change that will shield our weak fellow citizens for the first time. I think it is positive that the SYRIZA side too, despite the criticism that existed at the beginning of the application of the minimum guaranteed income, retained the minimum guaranteed income under another name, but maintained it. And today we are all here to make this big decision in favour of Greek citizens and our country. I urge you to vote in favour of the government's proposal. Thank you very much.

It is a moment when we are all called upon to consider how the development of the institutions and of the culture can be crystallised in the Constitution, the evaluation of the functioning of the institutions and provisions of the Constitution. It is a moment when political and social relation is reflected, as is expressed every time by popular sovereignty. This creates specific different data in this review process. Especially in this review process - although every time historical collective experience is taken into account - since the 2008 Review until now, in 2019, we can only take into account the great traumatic and dramatic collective experience of the crisis and the enforcement of the memorandums. The crisis in the Memorandum policies throughout Europe and in our country, which we have experienced dramatically, has had a direct impact on the functioning of the institutions, on the functioning of Parliament. It has led to the weakening of the institutions as a whole and the confidence of the people and the democratic institutions, and one of the biggest victims of this period was the social state and the social rights that we owe today to our debate to include them. This section is one of the most important. Unfortunately, even though the SYRIZA Parliamentary Group, already from the Proposed House, but also from other opposition parties, have tabled important provisions on rights as a whole and on social rights in particular, we see, on the part of the majority, that there is no will - and I shall refer to the arguments which have been heard in this Chamber - only the proposal for Article 21, which the majority has tabled on the proposal which the Minister for Labour has previously put forward, on a minimum guaranteed income. I believe, first of all, that with regard to Article 3, Article 13 and Article 5, the majority position is inconsistent, not with a left-wing proposal - it is not here - but with fundamental liberal provisions, when the majority determines itself as a European party, and it speaks of democratic modernisation in terms of our constitutional framework and the provisions which are included. In other words, when Article 3 states that the time has come for rationalisation - both our rapporteur and our special rapporteur have said so very well - between relations between States and the Church, this is a fundamental democratic proposal to modernise our constitutional framework. With regard to the inclusion of the admission of prevailing religion, this under no circumstances has Mr Kamine left - it has no regulatory dimension. That is why there is the interpretative statement, which quite clearly states that there is no regulatory dimension behind this wording, proposed for purely historical and accreditation reasons. So, in this sense, we think it is a democratic development for our own Constitution, and hence the political oath and oath of the President of the Republic. Where we find the majority totally inconsistent is with Article 5, because here we are talking about the elementary principle, the enrichment and updating of a fundamental individual right, which is the free development of the individual's personality. I mean, it's the basis of the urban constitutions, it's not about an ideological race. On the contrary, it shows conservatism on the part of the New Democracy, which is not justified by a liberal party, which has neoliberal views, unless we disagree with the neoliberal dimension of your policy. The proposal we are talking about and we have presented concerns the existence of absolute protection and non-discrimination on grounds of sex, gender and sexual orientation. This is something that you, ladies and gentlemen of the majority, have already voted on under the Treaty of Lisbon in 2008, as regards the Charter of Fundamental Rights. It is paragraph 21 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights, which is fully integrated and produces the same results as the Treaty of Lisbon, where it clearly states that ‘it prohibits any discrimination on grounds of sex and sexual orientation’. We cannot incorporate into our Constitution everything that we put or have accepted in European treaties. This could be said in terms of specific policy specificities, such as, for example, the policies of the Eurozone, where they do not need to be enshrined in the Constitution. However, as far as the basis of a constitution, namely individual and social rights, is concerned, that is not the case. We therefore believe that until we get to the vote you will have to look into this matter again. I now come to the most critical of all the issues, which are social rights. The truth is that since the 1990s, since 1989, with the full prevalence of unregulated globalisation without rules, we have gradually seen the financial system, markets and the economy dominated policy. However, we have seen something more serious, and here I would like to respond to the specific arguments which have been heard by majority speakers. We saw a political economic view, as neo-liberalism was, at the time, an economic view and not even the prevailing one. We have therefore seen the European Union's response to institutionalise it in a fragmentary way in the context of the functioning of the Eurozone, often in conflict with the Treaty of Lisbon. At the same time, we saw the disintegration of the social state and social rights, which was the great conquest of the social contract, which followed World War II with the next generation of social rights. This is a critical introduction to our specific proposals, which Mr Katrugalos and Mrs Ahtsoglou have presented, in order to respond to the arguments which have been made regarding these proposals and I quote one - one. First argument. We have heard it from Mr Javara, who always speaks very beautifully and documentedly, the rapporteur-general of the New Democracy, who said: 'But why do you need the extra safeguard? The Constitution in 1975 has guaranteed them." That is the one view, which has been heard many times by the majority. No one denys that the 1975 Constitution was promoted in relation to many other Social Rights Constitutions. But we now have another juncture, another experience, and that is why we cannot stay in that it was promoted at the time, but in what we have lived in these ten years and what has proved to be the case with regard to social rights. What has been proved, ladies and gentlemen, is that social rights were not shielded. The second argument I heard from the rapporteur-general of the Change Movement, Mr Loverdou, is that we should not include wishing words in the Constitution. I have to say that the dramatic experience of the Troika and the institutions was against the suffocating and blackmailing pressures accepted by all the governments that implemented memoranda, there was an important diversion when the ministers were talking to the troika and then to the institutions. And this impediment was the provisions of the Constitution. So the second argument on blessing is also being rejected, in that even because the troika that had a lack of democratic legitimacy and as the partners operated on all issues, when it was a matter of constitutional regulation, because they also represented a European constitutional system, the Constitution was a point in the negotiation. So that's going down, too. They're not wishing. When there is an explicit provision in the Constitution, it also regulates the application of these rights, but also negotiating on the basis of the recent experience of the country. Let's not close our eyes like we're in 2008 and the previous Review. It's been eleven dramatic years. We would be blind, I believe, if this is not the case, to ensure social rights. Furthermore - and this is the reality and the essence of the ideological and political difference - if the constitutional arguments are pretext. Then say, for your part, that you are in fact opposed to having another ideological position and orientation. For my part, I responded to the institutional arguments. With regard to the third argument, which my colleague, Ephie Ahtsoglou, replied. We have heard from the majority and from some opposition parties the question: 'How can they be put to the Constitution for Social Rights when they are in line with the country's budgetary possibilities'? But that's the reversal of the argument. The very argument is that neoliberalism and austerity policy have been institutionalised at European level, and it has been attempted by the majority, but it has been deterred by the proposed House, that there should be the corresponding regulation of the Eurozone as regards the budget and our Constitution, which would be the full constitutional introduction of a neoliberal restrictive policy. So the opposite is true, the rights exist and, as has been explained, there may be a relativeization of their implementation in periods of economic difficulties, but they cannot be abolished. Social rights cannot be disqualified. This introduction, ladies and gentlemen, is necessary and crucial to explain why, on our part, we insist on establishing them in the Constitution, and we consider that your refusal is mainly to do with a different ideological and political position and not with a different institutional constitutional view. This was understood that the Minister for Labour presented before the proposal for a'minimum guaranteed income'. We are proposing our own level of decent living in a universal social state, with universal social services. What is the risk, experience and feasibility behind this diversification? Where clean neoliberalism prevailed, because its effects had to be addressed, extreme poverty, spectacular inequalities and unemployment and the collapse of the social state. We have supported a universal social state which guarantees a decent living for citizens. The minimum guaranteed income approach does not cover us and we explain why. In the debate that took place in the Committee on Revision of the Constitution - there was a great deal of discussion - there was an opportunity for the majority to increase its proposal. We will see whether a comprehensive proposal will be tabled by Mr Javarra. In any case, until we see the intentions of the majority, in terms of including the universality of social services, we remain cautious, and we support our original proposal I mentioned earlier. I would like to mention something else to the Members of the majority who you have to say in the European Union. In the suspension of social rights, the first infringement took place at European level. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u161	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, in our first speech, we mentioned that the debate on the Revision of the Constitution is a pretext for the eyes of the world, since the only, substantial change is the vote of Greeks abroad, especially when the debate is taking place at a time when Greece is more than bankrupt, under the supervision of its creditors, at least until 2100 and with governments that sell everything at degrading prices, both public and private property. They haven't even hesitated to mortgage what we've got, and we don't have the criminal PSI. They outlawed the Constitution, reversed the referendum. They sold out Macedonia's name without even holding a referendum and abandoned our borders, so our islands sinking from illegal immigrants, with the settlement of our country on the ground by people who certainly do not want to assimilate, as the Minister of Civil Protection believes, but to assimilate us. However, the Greek Constitutions, according to the Greek law, were born in the flames of the Greek Revolution, after being hatched by the visions of the Greek Enlightenment. In particular, since the start of the fight of independence, the Greeks have been fascinating to manifest their will for real, democratic governance. Thus, during the revolution, three democratic constitutions were introduced. Sadly, however, they had to abandon them for the sake of survival. Then successive uprisings led to the adoption of five Constitutions: 1844, 1864, 1911, 1925 and 1927. Thus, our country was gradually led to the first Hellenic Republic by the Constitutions of 1925 and 1927. However, just eight years after the 1927 Constitution, the monarchy recovered again, while the war, German occupation and the Civil War led Greece back to the restoration of authoritarianism with the 1952 Constitution and the 1967 dictatorship. After the fall of the dictatorship now, Greece had the great opportunity for a genuine democratic reform, as well as for a radical renewal of political life, but without making it possible, after the same families have returned to power, with the political help of the generation of the Polytechnic, along with the old 1952 constitution most of it. It followed the anachronistic constitution of 1975, which became even worse with its subsequent revisions, a constitution that allowed the creation of the monster of post-multipleism, which lived - and lives - in parasitic terms, by destroying our public wealth. The final result was to lead Greece to the excessive debt of 2009, to the bankruptcy of 2012 and to the loss of its national sovereignty, without any reason at all, while the future that is being erased before us is much darker than we imagine, although there are many solutions available to us. In any case, our story has established that the Constitutions only change by revolution and never by those who use them to suck off everyone else. Let us hope, of course, that nothing will happen again, since everyone should have learned from their experiences, something that remains to be proved in practice. Furthermore, we must know that the political system consists of two things: institutions and political persons operating on these institutions. Once the institutions are loose and there are no institutional security barriers, then politicians, who are defects, like all of us, are fleeing from the task of defending national and social interests. Therefore, we need institutional security barriers so that political persons can operate exclusively in the national and social interest. However, such institutional differences do not exist in Greece, for example: Firstly, we do not have a constitutional court for quick and universal cancellation of an anti-constitutional law. Therefore, the government in question is easy to establish anti-constitutional laws, serving various interests. None of us understand why our country is the only, almost, in Europe, who does not have a constitutional court. Who really stops it? Secondly, we have no referendums on the citizens' initiative, so that the government in question fears that it can be faced with citizens if it works against them. Thirdly, we have not elected the President of the Republic by the citizens so that it can stand in the way of intentional or incorrect choices of a government. A recent example here is the President of Iceland, who rescued her from a fate similar to Greece. Fourthly, we have no distinction between executive, legislative and judicial authority. All powers are over-concentrated in the hands of a almighty Prime Minister, literally an elected monarch, which is a shame for all of us. Fifthly, the House, i.e. the current government majority, is still solely responsible for the crimes of ministers and Prime Ministers, not regular justice as it was due. Sixthly, it is still immediately removed from the hands of justice or any investigation of crime as soon as a minister's name has been reported. It is not possible for the political system to be finalised. In short, Article 86 is still working to cover any crimes of the ministers. Nothing changes what is being proposed. We could add a lot more, but it would take a lot of time that we don't have. We would just like to add that by listening to our SYRIZA colleague claiming that it is not the Constitution for the Crisis in the country, but that it is also the victim of the crisis, and also that the crisis has created a 'parallel', with the memorials etc., next to the Constitution, we have sincerely been disappointed. Simplifiedly, on the basis of what we have just said, the Constitution was not the victim of the crisis, but it has made a major contribution to the creation of the crisis, and it will then not be able to help save our country. For example, when SYRIZA violated the July 2015 referendum and made the 'no' of the citizens 'yes' for lenders, where was the Constitution to cancel this act and punish those responsible? Where was the Constitution to cancel the monuments and enslavements of the country for a century, punishing those responsible? Where was the Constitution to cancel the Embassy Agreement and punish those responsible? Where is the possibility for citizens to provoke a referendum to put order in the country? Where is the President of the Republic, with the power of election by the citizens, to ask for explanation from the government? So where is the Constitution for all this anticipated? [[Applause from the ward of Greek Solution]] So let us not deal here with articles that are not going to change absolutely anything, such as those that are being proposed. Let us do what we need to do if we want to be worthy of Greeks and of their history. Let's stop mocking them with pretext changes. Let us do something that really benefits our country, which experiences a unique crisis in world history. With regard to the articles of the first thematic section of the Constitution we are negotiating today, I would like to say that neutrality is not the same as freedom. Thus, the concept of religious neutrality does not match religious freedom. Our Constitution, however, protects religious freedom in Article 13(1), which, in fact, is not revised on the basis of Article 110(1) of the Constitution. In addition, the provision of Article 3, as it is shaped, does not imply religiously identifying the Greek state. The term 'predominant religion' has a real dimension. It is referred to as predominant in the sense that most of the population are Christian Orthodox, not in the sense that it surpasses, that it is in any way preceded by religious conscience. In Part Three of the Constitution, the organisation and functioning of the state, in particular in Section II, deal with issues relating to the President of the Republic. Article 33(2) of the Constitution explicitly provides that the President of the Republic is to give a religious oath with specific content. The Greek Solution believes that the President of the Hellenic Republic should give a religious oath only before taking up his duties. The duties of the President of the Republic shall give prestigious and supreme responsibility, which, by definition, only entail religious vows. We also believe that Article 59(1) of the Constitution, which provides for a religious oath for Members taking up duties. On the basis of all the above, we argue that Articles 3, 13(5), 33(2), 59(1) and 59(2) of the Constitution must remain the same. Now, on the second theme, I would like to say: This provision of the Constitution clearly writes "for absolute protection of life, honor and freedom". He writes, "Everyone who is on Greek territory". The phrase 'all those...', etc., obviously refers to everyone, regardless of sex, gender, age or anything else. The editorial legislator did not want to determine by aggressive definitions 'all', precisely so as not to restrict, not to exclude anything. In any event, the provision refers to fundamental rights. So, by definition, the phrase must be simple and general in order to ensure absolute protection. In conclusion, I would like to emphasise once again that our first priority must be the establishment of a constitutional court. At least for the Greek Solution this is the first priority. I shall mention just a few constitutions and exactly how they work. For example, the French Constitution in Article 61 states that the laws before they are published and implemented must be submitted to the Constitutional Council which decides on their agreement with the Constitution. The same applies to the Belgian court in 142: ‘The administrative court shall decide on the infringement of the articles of the Constitution by a decree...’, etc. Any person having a legitimate interest or a preliminary ruling may appeal to that court to any authority designated by the law. In the Swiss constitution we have Article 108, where every Swiss citizen, the national council, can be appointed to the federal court. In German we don't have a constitutional court alone in the federal state, but in every single federal state. Up to Skopje there is a constitutional court, composed of nine members among prominent members of the legal profession. Only Greece has no constitutional court. So someone's blocking it. Thank you very much.
