ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u184	Thank you, Mr President, and the Members who gave me their position so that I could speak before I went to the Chair. Ladies and gentlemen, this second theme session could be entitled 'The Lost Opportunity of Constitutional Review'. Today we are debating provisions which did not have the required majority of eighty-eight votes. These are very important provisions because they concern individual and social rights, provisions which characterise the quality of a liberal and democratic constitution. Liberal, however, in its true sense of freedoms, the recognition to the citizen of an arsenal of individual rights and fundamental freedoms, which will protect him from the violent intervention of the administration and power in its minimum individual space. Democratic because social rights enrich the production of society with the greatest possible participation of the wider majority, greater popular participation in economic, cultural and social life, thus more democracy in the country's political life. There is now an opportunity to broaden this liberal and democratic element of our Constitution with provisions that we have proposed and are in line with the new concepts that are formed in Greek and European society, with provisions that would be in line with our state history from the first constitutional formation to the provisional state of Epidaurus. The revolutionary Greeks two centuries ago did not hesitate to recognise a completely radical, for all of Europe, a range of rights and freedoms with the influence of the social demands of the Revolution, but also of the Greek and European Enlightenment. We are completely appalling when we are going to be harmonized in modern progressive and democratic perceptions. I'll stand in two places. The first is to extend our Constitution's liberal right to the individual area where power is forbidden. The majority view is that there should be no change in Article 5(2) of the Constitution to prohibit discrimination. Do we really believe that the article that exists today is satisfying us? Should not special attention be paid to banning discrimination, and in terms of gender, gender and sexual orientation, to the enjoyment of rights associated with the protection of life, honor and freedom? The reality proves that the protection that this article ensures today is inadequate and we have recent examples. As soon as day before yesterday, the Law of the Ministry of Sports was suspended, which provided for the application of a gender quota to the boards of the sports federations. In addition, the general wording of the first subparagraph of paragraph 2 of the Article shall be insufficient to guarantee the rights of refugees and migrants. We must remember that the enjoyment of equal rights for foreign and national citizens exists in our constitutional order from the constitutions of the Revolution. Freedom, which this article very rightly guarantees for foreigners within the Greek territory, can be limited when the possibility of broad interpretation is given to the exceptions introduced by international law, thus being invalidated in practice. Recent catalytic example of incorporating the directive of 13 December 2011 of the European Parliament on the status of international protection with unauthorised and anti-humanist restrictions. It is imperative to vote in favour of the change in this article in order to broaden the enjoyment of the rights of life, freedom and honor for all with a broad consensus. The Merra25 proposal covers precisely this need. The second opportunity we are losing is to confirm the democratic prerogative of the Constitution by deepening social rights, since the majority proposal is that there should be no change. In recent years, they have been characterized by a huge decline in social rights. The dreadful narrative of the "necessary status" was worked on without a time horizon. He also found a response to the case-law of the Supreme Courts. Today social rights have, I would say, a fig sheet. It is therefore imperative to reaffirm a number of rights once again: the right to work, health, social security, the deepening of the protection of motherhood, marriage, family and, in particular, the only parental family - children and the elderly. It is imperative to recognise new social rights, which are now matured, in our advanced society, such as the right to water, the enjoyment of electricity and the protection of first homes. Of course, how will you proceed to the full privatisation of PPC, water supply and red loans with the HERAKLIS project? I'm sorry, I forgot. It is very easy to recognise rights. It doesn't cost anything. The difficult thing is to guarantee that rights have meaning, content and application for everyone. Example: You guarantee digital governance. But can anyone, who doesn't have access to electricity and telecommunications, have access to a series of basic activities via the Internet? We recognize the social right to health. This is relatively easy. The difficult thing is to guarantee the right to water supplies, which ensures a minimum of hygiene, but also survival itself. Even if we vote on the most revolutionary and radical proposals on social rights, how can they find the ground for implementation, since the citizen is forced to get rid of them when the need for survival is pressing for poverty and impoverishment? Then we need constitutional protection. I do not have the illusion that a Western society today intends to guarantee the citizen an eight-hour job for which he can claim remuneration in proportion to his needs. But let us guarantee everyone the minimum: the right to survival, a minimum level of dignity. Let us put it down in the Constitution, let us establish a guarantee for benefits, so that no one can lose them to a degrading degree, as we are experiencing today. Let us make the right to work more imperatively and to collective agreements more urgently so that another development can be launched by designing labour-intensive investment, with decent conditions and fair remuneration. In the declaration of our party, we paraphrase our national poet with the words "the freedom of many will be excellence". That is the point of our realistic intervention in terms of the new progressive concepts accepted throughout Europe for banning discrimination. That is the point of our democratic intervention on social rights, which expresses the vision of hope for a society that is as many as possible involved. Today we must stand brave and not fearful, so that this historic opportunity is not lost. I urge you to vote in favour of the May25 proposal, a proposal which responds with a courageous and visionary 'yes' to the enlargement and deepening of social and individual rights. Because the Greek people need more freedom and deserve more democracy, two values that the Revolution of 1821 has translated throughout Europe and their shield in the Constitutional Review will be the best celebration of its two hundred years. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u185	Mr. George Papaliou has the floor from SYRIZA.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u186	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to refer to the unity of the social state and of social rights, which is also the very field of ideological political confrontation between the political forces, particularly in this respect between SYRIZA and the New Democracy. The SYRIZA proposal, of course, has an ideological character. The view of the colleagues of the New Democracy and many of them and the Movement of Change that social rights do not bring about anything essential in our proposal, but they are simply suggesting reprints, which have no practical impact, that this is verbatimism and the addition of useless words and phrases. In contrast to individual freedoms, social rights constitute an important element of the social state. In their constitutional capacity, they are given increased formality. The birth of social rights is linked to the fact that the social majority does not have access to basic goods. The few and the powerful are managed and fruitful of the produced wealth, since they possess the means of production. Thus, social rights aim to ensure that the majority of the social majority is equally involved in economic and social life. In their recognition rights and institutions - objective legal principles are guaranteed. This is why not only individuals and collective entities, but also the State which is obliged to act in specific directions and to pursue policies with corresponding content. Through social rights as collective action rights, wider areas where social competition is developing where interests conflict. For example, workplaces, social security, education, health and other social goods, the State is entitled to have, at least, the first or dominant or exclusive reason, in order to protect the public interest and to make the most of the socially fair distribution of the wealth produced. The protracted economic and social crisis has also had serious consequences for social rights. Unprecedented unemployment, particularly among young people, the deterioration of the protection of the world of work, the decline in the fields of social security and health, as well as the abolition of collective bargaining and collective agreements, have led to the degradation, substantial abolition of the social state and, consequently, of any social protection structures. This leads to the need to restore and strengthen social rights, which must be legally binding, in addition to the nature of their purely political declaration. In addition, the effectiveness of the social principles and rights guaranteed by constitutional law is also sought, as such protection is mainly achieved through legislative institutional intervention. SYRIZA's proposal highlights the progressive concept of a social state that is stable and strengthened with rights that will apply to all citizens. The aim is to strengthen the regulatory density, but also the conductivity of social rights. Furthermore, in our proposal, not only is the universality of social rights strengthened, but the social state is shielded in such a way as to minimise the risk that Greek society will experience such a and large poverty, which it suffered during the 'immemorandum' policies. In this context, indicative elements which make up our proposal are as follows: ensuring a decent standard of living for all citizens, through specific interventions and policies, public control of basic social goods, water and electricity, in such a way as to ensure that all citizens have access to them and that the relevant services are of high quality and, of course, at affordable prices - the position of cheap water shows, unfortunately, the Government's intentions - to guarantee the right to health not only for the State to provide health services but also for the right of citizens to have access to effective health services through the national health system, the application of the principle of equal treatment in the field of work and, consequently, the prohibition of any kind of age, which, during the period of the memorandums, has been cancelled by the legacy of the under-paid wages for young workers until the age of twenty-five years. I would like to stress, however, in the recent Energy Legislation, which is in the process of being discussed in the committee responsible, where the principle of equal pay for equal work is unequivocally fulfilled, by distinguishing between old and new workers. Enlargement of the protection of work, collective labour rights and the right to social security is one of the principles on which our proposal is based. Conservation of the collective autonomy of free collective bargaining, collective agreements and, in addition, the principles of favorability and unilateral referral to arbitration are proposals which are considered necessary, following the lamentation of the 'immemorandum' arrangements which have established labour law and left workers at the mercy of employers' arbitrage. This complements the constitutional framework for the protection of workers. On the contrary to SYRIZA's proposal, the neoliberal concept expressed by the New Democracy takes on social rights as mere wishes applied by the relevant state bodies in due course. In fact, social rights are seeking to be a kind of charity. And one last, as far as the rights of group action in the workplace are concerned. The trade union activity and the exercise of the right to strike for the government are regarded as dangerous, by pretext for the trade union bureaucracy, but also by trade union practices, as has recently been done by the Prime Minister who has denounced the right to strike as a cause of discomfort for the citizens. The continuing complaints of these rights by all market-controlled propaganda mechanisms are aimed at radically weakening the position of workers so that individual contracts of employment ultimately prevail. This, unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen of the Staff Regulations, is your pursuit. The version of the constitutional review you propose is inspired by the one and only concept that you recognize, that of neoliberalism. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u198	Let me make a general reference to the context in which this Constitutional Review is conducted. We are in the phase, ladies and gentlemen, during which the Greek society is struggling to get out of the ten-year crisis that led to the weakening of the national state's regulatory role as a result of globalisation, which led to the reduction of state interventions in the economy by allegedly returning to the self-regulatory role of the market. A crisis, during which, in the name of the competitiveness of the Greek economy, the dominant way of regulating labour disputes through the tripartite negotiation of a state, workers and employers was effectively abolished, the public sector fell further through the privatisations of previous state-owned enterprises or organisations, with risks to the constitutional rights themselves and, in particular, to equality in the use of public services and goods, society's weakest have been badly affected by the weakening of the old mechanisms of the welfare state and the general principle of national sovereignty has been considerably reduced, despite the fact that some do not want to hear it. However, this restriction was found to be lawful by the supreme courts of our country in view of compelling reasons of public interest relating to the exceptional nature of the budgetary crisis. I must point out, of course, that the caselaw of the Supreme Special Court has, in fact, been brought to the top of the time limits which can be recognized in such exceptional circumstances. In view of the circumstances in which this debate is being held, I think that it is easy or almost self-evident to answer the question whether it is appropriate to further strengthen the constitutional principle of the social state. Unlike the view on the adequacy of the constitutional-political proposals of the social-private constitution, the crisis has demonstrated the inadequacy of these regulations to explicitly establish, firstly, an inflexible and irreversible protectionist core of social-rights in the form of the relevant acquis and, secondly, the strict delimitation of the action of social-protection actors, in order to exclude the privatisation of social-policy. The experience of the crisis therefore requires the regulatory strengthening of social rights in order to address their dispute and regulatory scope as an institutional retreat to the neoliberal tendencies of the social state. Therefore, an approach of the Constitution that excludes neoliberal policies and tends to safeguard effective mechanisms of social solidarity and social justice cannot ignore either the changing socio-political reality, that is, our transition to post-industrial society, the community of the 4th Industrial Revolution, or the need to safeguard both the regulatory density of the provisions of social content and the legal certainty. Ladies and gentlemen, a country's constitution is not and cannot be ideologically neutral. The ideological neutrality of the Constitution does not exist. There is the tradition of the Anglo-Saxon constitution, where the constitution is essentially non-constitutional, that is, restrictive binding, generalist and vague. There is also the tradition of the central European constitutions, which also comes under the constitution of our country, where the Constitution is the fundamental law of the State, that is, the supreme rule of law which includes the regulatory principles of lastingity, that is, regulatory principles binding by each government. The question now arises: To what extent and in what form is it appropriate to revise the constitutional provisions on social content, in order to safeguard the mechanisms of social solidarity, adapted to the new circumstances? In this context, it was considered appropriate in the SYRIZA proposal to include in Article 21(1) the constitutional safeguarding of the notion of decent living for all. In determining the standard of living, the legislator must use scientific methods and take into account the current, objective and social conditions. Special protection is granted to family, marriage, maternity, children, young people, the elderly, the disabled and the most deprived. By adding this clause to the provisions of social rights in Article 21(1), the imperative nature of social rights is strengthened, by expressly referring them to the principle of human value, which is, moreover, the womb of all social rights and the most pressing of all the obligations of the State in accordance with Article 2(1). In other words, with the proposed new wording of Article 21(1), the provisions on the social rights of the Constitution have acquired their own regulatory and social constitution the most clear expression that it has ever had in our constitutional text, as the new provision is the equivalent of Article 5(1) in terms of individual rights and nonetheless strengthens the compellingness of all individual social rights by reference to Article 2(1), which is the womb of all social rights and the primary obligation of the state. In the same direction, i.e. strengthening social rights, the other proposals for specific regulations, namely Article 21(3), on the right to health, Article 22(5) on social security, Article 22(1), (2) and (4) on equal pay regardless of age, minimum wage and collective agreements. Our proposal, therefore, took into account the experience of the destructive crisis experienced by the country and on the basis of this experience, attempts to design on the basis of the lasting conditions appropriate to a constitutional review, those impurities and limits necessary to deal with immediate corresponding crises and in the future. I have chosen, ladies and gentlemen, in this section dealing with individual and social rights, to refer to the provision in paragraph 5 of Article 16, which may not be considered a reviewer, but which has monopolized the debate yesterday and today. Indeed, it is the reason why this Constitutional Review is considered by the Assembly to be of secondary importance and in any case, as a lost opportunity. I think that the New Democracy's proposal towards setting up private universities is in the wrong direction, because I think the first thing is to strengthen the self-government character of public universities, but not at the level of the Constitution, but at the level of the joint legislature. The first is to ensure the efficiency of the public university to provide this public good of university education and scientific development and, secondly, to examine, under strict conditions, whether there is room under the roof of the self-governed public university for the use of serious public initiatives by bodies in a specific legal form that suits the public-private character of the university. In no case, therefore, are we talking about anonymous commercial law companies that will build a seat, which will serve even better purposes under Article 16 of the Constitution. The critical of the university education provided by teaching and research is its definition of a public asset, namely to ensure that everyone, if they are able, irrespective of income or other obstacles, can have access to it. I would like to ask: is there anyone in this Chamber who disagrees? [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] The long-standing European tradition has therefore chosen the provider of this good to be the public, precisely to ensure the right of all those who want it and who have the ability to respond to the studies of this grade, without any other discrimination - income, racial - to have universal access to it. In all European countries, therefore, contrary to what is called or believed, the vast majority of university institutions are public universities and where they are not public, are non-profit-making organisations. Due to some traditions in some countries they belong to the Church, in some other countries they have been formed in the form of legacy. A system for providing the good of higher education which enables individuals to provide this public good, that is, without an effective system of scholarships for all those who need it and not some of them, is a system that catalyses the definition of university education as a public good. And I ask again: is there anyone who wants this? As I have already said, universities are institutions within which basic research is carried out and primary knowledge is produced. It is also crucial to maintain the public nature of the research, namely the free and equal access of everyone to its findings. This will ensure that the basic research is linked to production, and that economic operators also benefit as far as their competitiveness is concerned. The exclusion from the basic investigation therefore helps to dilute its comparative advantages for the benefit of certain companies by suspending the diffusion of the investigation findings to all operators of the production process.

The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights ensures the rights of minorities to defend their specific identity, the unique features that distinguish them from the other members of the human family. This is, in fact, the first sincere effort in the history of International Law to secure a universal right. In relation to the protection of religious integrity now in Greece, our country, although it has already accepted, since mid-1980, the right to individual recourse to the Strasbourg courts, continues on several occasions to deny diversity in religion, which many have seen in the European Court of Human Rights. The very close relationship between the Greek state and the Orthodox Church, as well as the intense migratory flow of recent years, have a catalytic effect on the formation of this negative result. The right of religious associations to self-determination and the duty of our country to abstain from interventions in their internal organization, the ECHR, the Court of Human Rights, in many cases, as well as that of the religious self-determination of the Muslim minority in Thrace in the Serif case against Greece. At this point, the court expressed an interesting case-law concerning the communities themselves, as associations and bodies of rights, worthy of self-protecting against State intervention. I am referring specifically to two presidential decrees by the former Minister of Education, Mr Gavroglou, which have undermined the value of self-determination against state intervention in relation to the model of administration of the mushrooms of Thrace, pretext of modernising them, creating a sense of deep intervention by the state in the operation of a well-known and recognised religion in our country. Furthermore, with regard to the nature of the right, religious freedom is sometimes founded as a repository, i.e. a claim for abstention of the State, which is obliged not to hinder or impede, otherwise, the exercise of the right and sometimes as a positive situation, which means that it creates a claim for the provision of the State, which is obliged to facilitate the incomplete and effective exercise of the right. After all, the preservation of religious autonomy within the framework of all legitimacy - always! - the protection of the language and the promotion of an advanced democratic and liberated, by national prejudices, of an educational system, is the aim of a modern European state. These are major issues and are directly linked to the protection of rights, but also the very nature of its existence. If, however, we really want to strengthen the constitutional safeguarding of religious freedom, we propose, as a Movement of Change, an addition of an interpretative statement in Article 3, which will expressly say that the reference to prevailing religion does not doubt the distinct role of State and Church and is not raised in Article 13(1), which would really solve the problem. And that's our proposal. Ladies and gentlemen, to conclude, I would like to say that it is necessary to make it clear in every way that in our country religious freedom is constitutionally guaranteed. Furthermore, the European Convention on Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, which Greece has ratified and enacted its domestic law, with superlegislative power, in fact, leave no room for other misinterpretations. But, through constitutionally regulated religious freedom, there is also the right to religious equality, i.e. equal treatment, regardless of religious beliefs, which also arises from the 'principle of equality', with which it is crucified. But for all this to apply, religious freedom must be specialised in practice daily. A series of laws and regulations are required, which state institutions and government machinery are required to implement. In this area, too, there are still problems which cannot be degraded or justified superficially. In a rule of law, all citizens must feel absolutely equal. And if some do not feel that, it is the state's duty and obligation to go into their problems, especially when it comes to the application of individual rights and, indeed, religious freedom. Thank you very much.

In Article 25, which refers to the principles of the social rule of law and the protection of fundamental rights, we have proposed adding a special amendment to the effects of artificial intelligence and the fourth industrial revolution, both to safeguarding and protecting human rights and to a fair social distribution of the wealth produced. The main objective of the amendment - intervention - was the added value and wealth produced by the technological revolution, not only by the elites who possess the information and algorithms of the applications, but also, as far as possible, obtain it from society as a whole. In addition, the wealth generated was fairly distributed to all States not only in tax havens, where the head office of the technological pools is usually located. In this direction, the European Commission and Parliament have already taken action with regulatory and control initiatives both dissuasive and punishing levels for technological collisions. In essence, this would constitute a new radical progressive, philosophical, ideological and political response to globalisation and modern capitalism. Instead of these, the now Professional Opposition found more ‘progressive’ and timely to deal with the review of Article 25(3) of the abuse of rights, in particular by removing the prohibition on the abuse of a right. This provision is crucial and cannot be removed because it has supported the weighting of conflicting rights, their harmonisation and democracy itself. Nor is it possible to invoke the expression 'not permitted' in Article 25(3) to apply Article 281 of the Civil Code as a general clause on individual rights. Because these are completely disparate cases: the provision of the Civil Code seeks to protect private interests, (particularly assets) from abuse of other, also private rights, so as to protect, on the presumption, the weakest part of the exploitation of the most powerful. Conversely, the provision of Article 25(3) of the Constitution seeks to protect the by definition and clearly stronger party, namely, the State authority, from exercising rights, which also tend by definition, to protect authority over it. Therefore, any direct or indirect legislative, administrative or judicial filling and specificisation of the term ‘not permitted’, which would result in any form of suspension or deprivation of the exercise of an individual right, would clearly be unconstitutional. Because the Constitution does not already provide for any other case of suspension of constitutional protection of individual rights, except that of Article 48. Nor is it permitted, with the current wording, to introduce exceptions or derogations from their constitutionally guaranteed protection. Moreover, such exceptions or derogations would also be contrary to the European Convention on Human Rights. This provision is therefore ‘incomplete’. It is lex imperfecta and does not involve sanctions, it is simply 'guidelines' to fill the meaning and interpretation of Article 5(1), 'if it does not insult the rights of others and violates the Constitution or good manners'. Ladies and gentlemen, we have called for the practical protection of the dignity of citizens by establishing a guaranteed standard of living, but also for the introduction of a provision that special law lays down the procedure and the conditions for obtaining housing assistance to those who are deprived of or resident inadequately. This is a guiding provision. It is more wishful, but symbolically useful and charged in particular 'as a lesson of crisis'. This provision, however, could even have the most powerful progressive content, insisting that all citizens legally live in Greece and not only Greek citizens. In Article 21(3) and (7) the former Professional Opposition has still proposed the "closing" of the public health system (paragraph 3) and public control, basic social goods (paragraph 7), which do not leave the necessary flexibility to regulate public and private relations to the legislator. I would like to remind you that both issues, health and social goods, the PASOK governments, were the ones that did not act and did not remain in the words. They adopted them and stayed in history. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] In Rule 22 - and I conclude, Mr President - a number of changes have also been proposed from the previous House. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u265	Thank you, Mr President. After a decade of containment on the one hand by the economic crisis and, on the other hand, the budget sizes of the central state, citizens expected a Review, which would go hand in hand with their expectations of having a share of prosperity in the new era and being run by a state that is effective, dynamic and flexible. The provisions which could introduce an innovation, such as new forms of direct democracy, unfortunately remain incomplete. The dialogue that should be sparked into the Greek society for the Revision of the Constitution has never begun, so that the entire review process remains within doors and outside society, and we as parliamentarians at this time, instead of meeting the needs of the future, are politically unseen. In addition, the short period of preparation of the Review Committee and the debate on this important state project demonstrates an unnecessarily urgency for the government to end with this parliamentary obligation as soon as possible. Based on the above data, I fully identify with the assessment of the general speaker of the Change Movement, Mr Loverdou, that the project of this Review is another lost parliamentary opportunity without boldness and vision. Therefore, following the political tradition of PASOK defended by acts of democracy, reforms, progress and individual and social rights, the Change Movement tabled concrete proposals considering the review project as a unique opportunity for a brave change in areas such as strengthening the independence of justice and the rule of law, upgrading the legislative work, establishing the Board of Civil Heads, controlling the parties' economics, dealing with climate change, non-state, non-profit universities. Especially in terms of the latter, while in most countries in Europe and here near Cyprus there are State and non-State universities, Greece once again lost the train. Our refusal to self-evidently, despite the attempt of the Change Movement to review Article 16 of the Constitution, undeniably takes the country backwards. In this respect, we have repeatedly given our examinations as a party to the incorporation of the right to social security, the protection of the family and of the health of citizens who are the main pillars of the social state as set out in Article 21 of the Constitution. Ensuring minimum guaranteed income is extremely important if we consider that in the years of the crisis overnight economic planning has been overturned and the percentage of households living below the poverty level has doubled. Of course, the national legislature must, in accordance with this constitutional requirement, determine the income limit for decent living both at wage and pension level and take the necessary measures to ensure it. We therefore expect to see at what level the introduction of the minimum guaranteed income will be transformed into national legislation in the case-law of the courts and generally targeted social benefits. In addition, the safeguarding of the right to health consists of the constitutionally established right of man to provide the State with quality health services. In other words, the state is obliged to take positive action to ensure the prevention and treatment of physical and mental health. It is up to the national legislator to specialise this right each time with concrete measures and initiatives. The constitutional guarantee of the right to health is clearly in the right direction. In the years of the crisis, however, it was the health sector that suffered as much budgetary adjustment as possible. This is demonstrated by the substantial freezing of the recruitment of permanent staff to the ESS for a decade, by the towel of credits to public hospitals for the supply of medicines and materials, by the residual functioning of psychiatric health structures, by the inability to finance primary health care, by the transfer of much of the cost to the medicine from the State to the citizen, by the failure to complete e-prescription, by the failure to implement the e-patient file. So all this health problems are due to what? Insufficient state funding. How, then, will this constitutional requirement be fulfilled if the government does not generously fund the health sector? In addition, it is not a constitutional health protection unless increased protection measures are adopted by the national legislature for older people, for multitudes and for disabled people. In a country that is ageing, with young scientists leaving the country, with a large number of migrants protecting the old age, luxury, people with special problems, is a necessity. Therefore, in order to protect the health of citizens, there is no need for constitutional wishes but for political will to finally take place. Finally, I am in favour of protecting public goods. I think it goes without saying that basic social goods such as water, electricity and hydrocarbons and, in general, the country's natural wealth must be in public control and the state must ensure that all citizens can have access to them. Ladies and gentlemen, the constitutional provisions must be simple, clear, so that they do not become vacant words and words. We take the political responsibility to support the provisions that we consider to be in the right direction and ensure political stability and strong democracy, and in this context we will also give our vote. Thank you.

In the light of what has been said, there has been a feeling that we are once again losing an important opportunity to revise provisions requiring real review and substantial interventions outside the micro-party interests. After all, the Constitution is the supreme legal text of regulation not only of the country's political life, but of the system of principles and values. Our prolongation, the Change Movement, has tabled a package of proposals for revision of constitutional provisions in the direction of upgrading the state's institutional functioning, strengthening citizens and consolidating the rule of law. Unfortunately, artificial polarization between SYRIZA and New Democracy and the prevalence of climate of post-court settlements overshadowed the debate on the constitutional review, largely cancelling the historic value of the project. As a result of the debate, the historic opportunity for necessary sectors and brave steps forward, which were included in the proposals of the Change Movement, some of which concerned, I say, Article 24 on climate change. Our prolongation has proposed the introduction of the state's obligation to adopt actions to tackle climate change and to protect biodiversity. Article 24, however, remains out of review. Article 25, new technologies and fourth industrial revolution: We proposed that the Constitution should provide for actions to safeguard human rights and a fair distribution of the wealth produced in the modern environment of technological development and artificial intelligence applications. These areas, of course, also touch on the primary sector, the rural development sector, which I have the honour of addressing. However, let me focus on provisions which are part of the revised provisions and which are part of the wider theme of family institutions, marriage, maternity and childhood, as well as the rights of people with disabilities. These are issues which concern and raise a very wide part of our society. These provisions - in this case Article 21 of the Constitution - fall within the framework of the protection of human and social rights. There has been a lot of talk and a lot of ink has been done to protect these rights, which must not only be guaranteed constitutionally and legislatively, but essentially, through government policies and directions. These provisions lay down the framework for the protection of the freedoms of individuals, their rights according to the family situation, their social status, their particular biological and mental abilities. They are, in other words, setting a framework for the existence and coexistence of the individual in a democratic society, making it a community of democracy. In short, these articles cannot remain empty. They should always be a guide to policy making in a progressive and philosophical direction. So we are positive about the revision of Article 21(1) as a government obligation to guarantee minimum guaranteed income as a minimum level of decent living, and that is because not only is it an essential axis of our policy of prolonging the impotence, but above all because it must be shared with the institutions of the State the importance that the constitutional legislator attaches to this state obligation. Furthermore, when it comes to guaranteeing social rights to health, we are not negative in Article 21(3). Health is a very important real human condition and it directly refers to the relationship between healthy man and prevention. Finally, we agree that the addition to Article 21 of a new provision as paragraph 7, which will protect public goods such as water, should of course not be understood as a guarantee of bureaucracy and the interests built upon it. The question, of course, is whether these interventions actually promote the Constitution as a more progressive direction by giving added value to individual and social rights. Are individual and social rights strengthened in the protection of the social state and the rule of law? Do they give a substantial signal to the need for a more progressive society that embraces diversity? Do they aim to create a state welfare, social justice and fair services mechanism? The reflection should therefore be strong for all of us today as to whether the review process in which we are involved is necessary, useful and beneficial for the present and future of the country. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the opportunity to revise the constitutional text, the absolute regulator of the policies of the respective government, provisions relating indeed to the citizen and the quality of his everyday life, unfortunately, has been lost. Provisions relating to bureaucracy in the country, the bureaucracy that is possible for administration and for justice remain untouchable. This review process does not care about the suffering that the citizen suffers as a persecutor, and that is why he unfortunately remains within Parliament's doors. It should be made clear, however, that this responsibility is borne by the sterile political controversy which took place in the previous House, which actually did not dare to focus on the real and burning political and social issues. He was caught in closed interventions to facilitate the respective system of power. The role of the Constitution, however, is super-party and must provide solutions, defending social goods and acquired assets, strengthening political stability, guaranteeing Greece's status as a modern European country on the international map. The new Constitution should recognise the need to adapt to the new digital age, to changing the social structures of refugee flows, to threatened climate change, to new economic and business events. In this light, the Constitution should be what always preserves the right of the worker, the unemployed, the luxury, the disabled person, to enter a new advanced society. Thank you very much.

Ladies and gentlemen, the debate on the Constitutional Review is one of the crowns of our parliamentary action, and that is why it requires an increased effort for all of us to work constructively to take advantage of this opportunity. With today's debate, as with those that will follow, we come to a constant reflection of the evolution of the culture, the material corrections and additions to the constitutional text, the need to regulate issues arising from reality and social needs. Above all, the issue of the political fixation of the Constitution, to make a reference to the recent past, is its mistreatment due to the Memorandum of Understanding and Cheques. We have lived a period which, thanks to the sacrifices of the people and the policy followed by SYRIZA, is now a long time ago, when the constitutional reports have accepted, to say discreetly, significant pressures to serve internal and external-cycle requirements. With this experience we had to go out more wisely and shield the Greek Constitution with a regulatory authority that would ensure even more social and individual rights than antidemocratic practices. If we want to return fully to an era of institutional normality, we must shield our society, political practice and therefore the constitutional charter with regulatory references that broaden democracy and citizenship, strengthen equality, protect the whole population against injustice and discriminatory behaviour. Why, in order to make the connection to today's unity, which mainly concerns the protection of work, but also other fundamental rights, can one of the majority Members who are absent from the Chamber today, as you can see, if the introduction of the subordinate wage for young people under the age of twenty-five years which was made by the governments of the New Democracy and PASOK in 2012 is constitutionally consistent with Article 22(1), which stipulates that all workers, regardless of sex or other discrimination, have the right to an equal pay for work of equal value? Or is it correct to exclude sectors and workers from collective labour agreements, provided for in the development law that you have recently voted on? We were critical and condemned by the relevant Council of Europe body to monitor the European Social Charter as a country of criticism and condemnation. In this context we have proposed that there should be an explicit guarantee in the Constitution that the provision for equal pay and equal work should be mentioned regardless of age so that young people will not be treated again as second-class citizens. Unfortunately you did not agree on this proposal. I just hope you don't have any thoughts of restoring this shameful provision. At this point in general, there is also the significant difference in our perceptions. You think even if you cover it in detail sometimes that the state should not intervene anywhere, that it should not commit society with restrictions and controls, even if it concerns checks on compliance with laws and rules that protect citizens' rights. We support the fact that there is a sphere of rights which are inalienable between them and social rights. These are the rights which fall within the particular section we are debating today, namely the right to personal freedom, to the protection of family, marriage, maternity and childhood, the rights of disabled persons and the protection of the working and social rule of law. These rights, I would say, are at the heart of the rights that the state must protect in order to strengthen the free will of its citizens. Why can an impoverished citizen, a citizen without access to health, social security and work be free from the environmental restrictions? My answer, as I hope most, is negative. Or, as our rapporteur says, in the constitutional review, the essence of our confrontation is that we regard social rights as a precondition for the autonomy of people. We therefore support freedom without protection of rights, so without a definition of the protection measures that the Constitution must describe is an empty meaning. That is why we want a Constitution that is more regulatory, descriptive and social law, a Constitution not regulating social relations, as we are accused of, but also a report of ideas, a constitution that will incorporate lessons from recent social and political history without evoking into a factual and consumable record of the context. That is why we are careful, but not out of date in the wording of our positions, and that is why we are seeking consent. However, there are examples which concern us about the mutual exercise of consensus required by the constitutional review. For example, we have made a clear proposal in Article 5(2) to incorporate the ban on discrimination based on sex or sexual orientation. However, the New Democracy has refused to vote in favour of this proposal. Did he consider that discrimination based on sex or discrimination resulting from sexual choices is a fashion, an excess supported by the majority rapporteur, so does not have to defend the Constitution? He wanted this to flatter the most conservative reflexes of society and parliament, I would say? Whatever the motive, the answer is disappointing for the world that believes in the freedom of the individual and his choices. We have also proposed that the public audit of basic social goods, such as water, electricity and their distribution networks, should be effectively safeguarded. Unfortunately, the New Democracy rejected the proposal because it supports the privatisation of social goods, as was seen by the recent increases in PPC tariffs from 15% to 20%. All this leads to the depreciation of the business. In conclusion, I should like to mention briefly the concern we have shown for a more substantial amendment to Article 21(1), which refers to the constitutional provision for minimum guaranteed income. Let me remind you here that we had made a proposal for a law since 2005 and had been rejected. We, although this provision is legitimate and necessary, have proposed that it be deepened by reference to the question of access to social services. For example, people with disabilities face many problems of equal access that are not just financially. I know that, for many years now, he has been a member of the Paralympic Committee and I know that it is not enough to have an intervention with a subsidy policy, but we need a holistic approach that includes structures, social services, access to public space, culture, sport, flexible working relationships and so many other elements that favour the integration of people, which are now, in one way or another, excluded from the public space and from the public sphere. The text of the revised article is a principle, but, in my opinion, it is incomplete and cowardly. We could do more. Ladies and gentlemen, the Revision of the Constitution is a historic moment, a celebration of democracy which our ancestors defended with struggles the last of which, the struggle of the Polytechnics, is the most recent one as a memory and experience. I call on you in this light of the defence and deepening of democracy to address the issue of reform on more progressive terms by giving the signal to a society that expects us to express that legislative power is lamenting its concerns and dreams for a better life, for a tomorrow inclusive and marginalisation. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-19-S1-commons.u357	Ladies and gentlemen, the timing of the constitutional review we are discussing these days has its own features, which are certainly related to the country's deep and protracted economic crisis, its multiple links with the crisis of political institutions, inequality and attacks on rights. I believe that SYRIZA's attitude to constitutional reform has been guided by these principles since the beginning of the previous parliamentary term. The very nature of our proposals with the three axes as determined, namely, the deepening and widening of democracy, secondly, the resurgence of neoliberalism and the strengthening of social rights, and thirdly, the restoration of confidence in the political system, marks our clear ideological and political position on the various issues, without any evasion. Of course, the attitude of the New Democracy, both during the previous parliament, as well as during the current, Revising Parliament, has effectively blocked any change other than a few articles, moving between conservative logics and attitudes and atmosphericity. Let me come to the first thematic section of the Constitutional Review, namely the one concerning relations between the Church - State, as described in Article 3. Our proposal, as we know, lies in guaranteeing the religious neutrality of the state. At the same time, the term "predominant religion" is maintained, but it is explained in our proposed interpretative statement that it is purely of an indictment and does not signify any discrimination in other doctrines. I consider this to be an extremely balanced proposal towards the clear strengthening of the distinction between the roles of the Church - State - which is extremely weak in relation to not only the European but also the global rule. On the one hand, this proposal recognises the historical and cultural weight that Orthodoxy has for the identity and collective life of the Greeks, on the other hand, that the Greek state is to be guaranteed as a religious neutral state, a state, that is to say, that is, it will not have a public religion, nor will it be legally prosperous towards a particular doctrine, no matter how strong it is to the majority of citizens. The New Democracy and, unfortunately, the Change Movement, which otherwise accuses us of lack of progressiveity, wonders what the reason for such a change in the settlement that has been historically achieved between the Church and the State. Ladies and gentlemen, despite the safeguarding of religious freedoms in Article 13 of the Constitution, it is obvious that historically a strong interdependence has been established so far between State and Church, which obviously creates unfavourable conditions for some citizens who belong to other doctrines or religions or who do not religion. As has often been pointed out, in the Greek case, it was the State that put the Church under its close supervision, effectively limiting its autonomy, but, as we all know, the Church gradually took advantage of this privileged position. Moreover, like the recent decisions of the Council of State on the teaching of Religiouss, it suggests that the culture of consciousness in schools is confused, precisely on the basis of the provision of Article 3, with the teaching of a confession course. We must not forget, too, that about 20% of the decisions of the European Court of Human Rights concerning violations of religious freedoms are concerned, unfortunately, only with one state, Greece. An argument that is not expressly expressed usually, but implied, is about the possible weakening of religious emotion. In this regard, it is sufficient to see that, on the basis of international research, such as Eurobarometer, religious faith in the other states of Europe with official religion, Britain, Denmark, Norway, is clearly weaker than the European average. On the contrary, in countries that do not have official religion, such as Ireland, Italy, Romania, Russia, the religious feeling is very strong. In other words, the strength or not of religious conscience has nothing to do with official religion in a state. Finally, with regard to the correlation between Article 3 and national identity, a question which I have seen quite clearly by the Special Rapporteur of the New Democracy in the Review Committee, let me consider it an extremely weak argument. Apart from the fact that indirectly it leads to the position that non-orthodox or non-religious Greek citizens fall short of patriotism and strong national conscience, they cannot answer why in states with religious or dogmatic disparity, such as, for example, in the United States, Germany or even in countries with relatively increased percentages of citizens who say they are atheist, such as the Czech Republic, Sweden, national identity remains strong. Here, let me not forget the participation of other Greek citizens, such as Jews, black Greek citizens in the patriotic struggles of the Albanian Front and the National Resistance. For all these reasons, I think that the apparent non-voting of our proposal is a lost opportunity to strengthen the distinct roles of the Church and State, which would be beneficial to both institutions and also to the Greek society in general. Thank you.

Not to mention extensively the Greek period, Solon, Klisthei or the "Corpus Juris Civilis" of Justinian, which is the foundation of the organization and functioning of modern Western states, the constitutional history of our country is linked to the course of the Greek nation already since the pre-revolutionary period and the "New Civilian Administration", in 1797, the nationalist witness of our people and all the Balkan peoples, King Velestini. The preamble to the current constitution, which is already in progress from the Constitution of Epidaurus, but also Article 3, are not entirely anachronistic remnants, as it sees a school of thought, a western account of the historical evolution of the nation and its institutions. Nor are they, of course, an anti-historic and conservative view, such as that of the New Democracy party - and the Right in general - on this issue. On the contrary, they are part of the particular historically shaped, Greek idiosyncrasies, our national and social traditions within the general movement of history. Otherwise, the Greek Revolution was first national liberatory and not socio-retrospective, as, for example, the French Revolution, although it had this dimension. In other words, he defined the horizons of the Greek Constitution. The Constitution of the Kypronamenos III Hellenic Republic, which seals it indelibly, has been formed with the revisions of 1986, 2001 and 2008. The latest review is more important for the article that has not been revised, the famous article 16 on public free education in higher education and which, in fact, has illustrated the dynamic and movemental participation and presence of the folklore as an actor subject to the constitutional review process. Since 2010, with the monumental subordination of the country, its institutional state independence and its guaranteed self-determination since 1975 has been critically affected. The country's dependence has been placed on the institutional structures, production relations and the functioning of the legal structure. Control is now being carried out in more modern and effective terms, establishing, on the one hand, the degradation of the independence of the country, on the other hand, the economic violence of the system in forms that are particularly intense. For several aspects of the memorandum policies relating to social and economic rights, such as pensions and subsistence, there have been decisions by the Council of Europe responsible for monitoring compliance with the European Social Charter and they have considered it to be infringing it. So, having the specific experience, which has been experienced by Greek society since 2010, SYRIZA in the proposal for revision, particularly in the articles that I will be referring to and which we are talking about today about about social rights, attempts to give a constitutional grid of protection to the vital cells of Greek society that were ruthlessly struck in the years of the crisis. For example, I mention the amendment - an improvement in paragraph 2 of Article 5 that we propose to ban discrimination on grounds of sex and sexual orientation, which the New Democracy rejects. Obviously gender equality for the New Democracy is a right that should not be constitutionally guaranteed, and we are entitled to ask ourselves what century they live in. With Article 21(1), we, as SYRIZA, move towards the constitutionalisation of a universal social system, where the state guarantees through universal social services and income support a decent standard of living for all, unlike the corporatist model or, even worse, the remnant - liberal, according to the international typology of the welfare state, which New Democracy is proposing in its proposal. In Article 21(3), we are proposing that it be reviewed by a general reference which is now available for care, guarantee and obligation by the State for universal access to effective health services through the National Health System. These are rights - public goods, for which we believe there should be a follow-up to their provision at affordable prices and high quality. In this context, I mention, for example, the 1906/2014 plenary session of the Council of State, which considered the privatization of the EEAS to be unconstitutional and canceled the transfer of the latest shares of the EEAS to the JAPD. With our proposal, the inalienable nature of these lasting resources for the life and well-being of the citizens is constitutionally guaranteed at a time when privatizations and, in particular, the pro-investing way of the New Democracy Government fits in with what modern political theory calls "symbolisation by expropriation". We do not want Greece to become a colony and we want to safeguard the natural, socially responsible resources. Obviously, the New Democracy does not want this regulation, because it might want to bring it back to the future. In Article 22(2), we have proposed the constitutional safeguarding of basic principles of collective labour law, which, during the crisis, were severely affected, such as the principle of more favourable regulation, the ban on the overrun of minimum wages, laid down by collective agreements of employment, and the right to refer to arbitration.
